.navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}html.skin-theme-clientpref-night .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}@media(prefers-color-scheme:dark){html.skin-theme-clientpref-os .mw-parser-output .navbar li a abbr{color:var(--color-base)!important}}@media print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Business_administration" title="Template:Business administration"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Business_administration" title="Template talk:Business administration"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Business_administration" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Business administration"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Trade</b> involves the transfer of <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_services" title="Goods and services">goods and services</a> from one person or entity to another, often in exchange for <a href="/wiki/Money" title="Money">money</a>. Economists refer to a <a href="/wiki/System" title="System">system</a> or network that allows trade as a <a href="/wiki/Market_(economics)" title="Market (economics)">market</a>.
</p><p>Traders generally negotiate through a medium of credit or exchange, such as money. Though some economists characterize <a href="/wiki/Barter" title="Barter">barter</a> (i.e. trading things without the use of money<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Samuelson1939&#95;1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Samuelson1939-1"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>) as an early form of trade, <a href="/wiki/History_of_money#Emergence_of_money" title="History of money">money was invented</a> before written history began. Letters of <a href="/wiki/Credit_(finance)" class="mw-redirect" title="Credit (finance)">credit</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paper_money" title="Paper money">paper money</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Digital_currency" title="Digital currency">non-physical money</a> have greatly simplified and promoted trade as <b>buying</b> can be separated from <b>selling</b>, or <a href="/wiki/Earnings" title="Earnings">earning</a>. Trade between two traders is called <a href="/wiki/Bilateral_trade" title="Bilateral trade">bilateral trade</a>, while trade involving more than two traders is called <a href="/wiki/Multilateral_treaty" title="Multilateral treaty">multilateral trade</a>.  
</p><p>In one modern view, trade exists due to specialization and the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_labor" class="mw-redirect" title="Division of labor">division of labor</a>, a predominant form of <a href="/wiki/Economic_activity" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic activity">economic activity</a> in which individuals and groups concentrate on a small aspect of production, but use their output in trade for other products and needs.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-DollarKraay2004&#95;2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DollarKraay2004-2"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Trade exists between regions because different regions may have a <a href="/wiki/Comparative_advantage" title="Comparative advantage">comparative advantage</a> (perceived or real) in the production of some trade-able goods&#160;&#8211;&#32;including the production of scarce or limited natural resources elsewhere. For example, different regions' sizes may encourage <a href="/wiki/Mass_production" title="Mass production">mass production</a>. In such circumstances, trading at <a href="/wiki/Market_price" class="mw-redirect" title="Market price">market price</a> between locations can benefit both locations. Different types of traders may specialize in trading different kinds of goods; for example, the <a href="/wiki/Spice_trade" title="Spice trade">spice trade</a> and <a href="/wiki/Grain_trade" title="Grain trade">grain trade</a> have both historically been important in the development of a global, international economy. <a href="/wiki/Retail" title="Retail">Retail</a> trade consists of the <a href="/wiki/Sales" title="Sales">sale</a> of goods or <a href="/wiki/Merchandise" class="mw-redirect" title="Merchandise">merchandise</a> from a fixed location,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/E-commerce" title="E-commerce">online</a> or <a href="/wiki/Mail_order" title="Mail order">by mail</a>, in small or individual lots for direct <a href="/wiki/Consumption_(economics)" title="Consumption (economics)">consumption</a> or use by the purchaser.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-fas&#95;4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fas-4"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Wholesale" class="mw-redirect" title="Wholesale">Wholesale</a> trade is the traffic in goods that are sold as merchandise to <a href="/wiki/Retailer" class="mw-redirect" title="Retailer">retailers</a>, industrial, commercial, institutional, or other professional <a href="/wiki/Business" title="Business">business</a> users, or to other wholesalers and related subordinated services.
</p><p>Historically, openness to <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> substantially increased in some areas from 1815 until the outbreak of World War I in 1914. Trade openness increased again during the 1920s but collapsed (in particular in Europe and North America) during the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a> of the 1930s. Trade openness increased substantially again from the 1950s onward (albeit with a slowdown during the <a href="/wiki/1973_oil_crisis" title="1973 oil crisis">oil crisis of the 1970s</a>). Economists and <a href="/wiki/Economic_historians" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic historians">economic historians</a> contend that current levels of trade openness are the highest they have ever been.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Etymology"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><i>Trade</i> is from <a href="/wiki/Middle_English_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle English language">Middle English</a> <i>trade</i> ("path, course of conduct"), introduced into English by Hanseatic merchants, from <a href="/wiki/Middle_Low_German_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Low German language">Middle Low German</a> <i>trade</i> ("track, course"), from <a href="/wiki/Old_Saxon_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Saxon language">Old Saxon</a> <i>trada</i> ("spoor, track"), from <a href="/wiki/Proto-Germanic_language" title="Proto-Germanic language">Proto-Germanic</a> <i>*tradō</i> ("track, way"), and cognate with <a href="/wiki/Old_English" title="Old English">Old English</a> <i>tredan</i> ("to tread").
</p><p><i><a href="/wiki/Commerce" title="Commerce">Commerce</a></i> is derived from the <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> <i>commercium</i>, from <i>cum</i> "together" and <i>merx</i>, "merchandise."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
rvkdharwadglish by Hanseatic merchants, from <a href="/wiki/Middle_Low_German_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Low German language">Middle Low German</a> <i>trade</i> ("track, course"), from <a href="/wiki/Old_Saxon_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Saxon language">Old Saxon</a> <i>trada</i> ("spoor, track"), from <a href="/wiki/Proto-Germanic_language" title="Proto-Germanic language">Proto-Germanic</a> <i>*tradō</i> ("track, way"), and cognate with <a href="/wiki/Old_English" title="Old English">Old English</a> <i>tredan</i> ("to tread").
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_world" title="Economic history of the world">Economic history of the world</a> and <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_international_trade" title="Timeline of international trade">Timeline of international trade</a></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Prehistory">Prehistory</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Prehistory"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Trade originated from <a href="/wiki/History_of_communication" title="History of communication">human communication</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prehistoric" class="mw-redirect" title="Prehistoric">prehistoric</a> times. Prehistoric peoples exchanged goods and services with each other in a <a href="/wiki/Gift_economy" title="Gift economy">gift economy</a> before the innovation of modern-day currency.  Recent research finds evidence that early humans developed trade networks for obsidian 15,000 years ago as well as ostrich egg shell beads 50,000 years ago.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Padilla-Iglesias,&#95;Cecilia&#95;9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Padilla-Iglesias,_Cecilia-9"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Blegen,&#95;Nick&#95;10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blegen,_Nick-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Miller&#95;&amp;&#95;Wang&#95;(2021)&#95;11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Miller_&amp;_Wang_(2021)-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the Mediterranean region, the earliest contact between cultures involved members of the species <i>Homo sapiens</i>, principally using the Danube river, at a time beginning 35,000–30,000 <a href="/wiki/Before_Present" title="Before Present">BP</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Abulafia,&#95;Rackham,&#95;Suano&#95;12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abulafia,_Rackham,_Suano-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="Quotation needed from source to verify. (September 2019)">need quotation to verify</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Caduceus.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Caduceus.svg/120px-Caduceus.svg.png" decoding="async" width="110" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Caduceus.svg/250px-Caduceus.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="299" data-file-height="356" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Caduceus" title="Caduceus">caduceus</a>, traditionally associated with <a href="/wiki/Mercury_(mythology)" title="Mercury (mythology)">Mercury</a> (the Roman patron-god of merchants), continues in use as a symbol of commerce.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Attic_red-figure_Pottery_in_the_Eremitage_Sankt_Petersburg.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Attic_red-figure_Pottery_in_the_Eremitage_Sankt_Petersburg.jpg/250px-Attic_red-figure_Pottery_in_the_Eremitage_Sankt_Petersburg.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Attic_red-figure_Pottery_in_the_Eremitage_Sankt_Petersburg.jpg/500px-Attic_red-figure_Pottery_in_the_Eremitage_Sankt_Petersburg.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3248" data-file-height="2280" /></a><figcaption> <a href="/wiki/Etruscan_art" title="Etruscan art">Ancient Etruscan</a> "<a href="/wiki/Aryballoi" class="mw-redirect" title="Aryballoi">aryballoi</a>" terracota vessels unearthed in the 1860s at Bolshaya Bliznitsa tumulus near <a href="/wiki/Phanagoria" title="Phanagoria">Phanagoria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Southern_Russia" title="Southern Russia">South Russia</a> (formerly part of the <a href="/wiki/Bosporan_Kingdom" title="Bosporan Kingdom">Bosporan Kingdom</a> of <a href="/wiki/Cimmerian_Bosporus" class="mw-redirect" title="Cimmerian Bosporus">Cimmerian Bosporus</a>, present-day <a href="/wiki/Taman_Peninsula" title="Taman Peninsula">Taman Peninsula</a>); on exhibit at the <a href="/wiki/Hermitage_Museum" title="Hermitage Museum">Hermitage Museum</a> in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Saint Petersburg</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>There is evidence of the exchange of <a href="/wiki/Obsidian" title="Obsidian">obsidian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Flint" title="Flint">flint</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Stone_Age" title="Stone Age">Stone Age</a>. Trade in obsidian is believed to have taken place in <a href="/wiki/New_Guinea" title="New Guinea">New Guinea</a> from 17,000 BCE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1244412712">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 32px}.mw-parser-output .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;margin-top:0}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .templatequotecite{padding-left:1.6em}}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The earliest use of obsidian in the Near East dates to the Lower and Middle paleolithic.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></p></blockquote><div class="templatequotecite"><p style="display: inline; padding-left: 2.3em;">—&#8202;<a href="/wiki/Takahito,_Prince_Mikasa" title="Takahito, Prince Mikasa">HIH Prince Mikasa no Miya Takahito</a><span style="display:none" data-plural="0"></span></p></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Robert_Carr_Bosanquet" title="Robert Carr Bosanquet">Robert Carr Bosanquet</a> investigated trade in the Stone Age by excavations in 1901.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The first clear archaeological evidence of trade in manufactured goods is found in south west Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Archaeological evidence of obsidian use provides data on how this material was increasingly the preferred choice rather than <a href="/wiki/Chert" title="Chert">chert</a> from the late Mesolithic to Neolithic, requiring exchange as deposits of obsidian are rare in the Mediterranean region.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-John&#95;Robb&#95;24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Robb-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Obsidian provided the material to make cutting utensils or tools, although since other more easily obtainable materials were available, use was exclusive to the higher status of the tribe using "the rich man's flint".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Obsidian has held its value relative to flint.
</p><p>Early traders traded Obsidian at distances of 900 kilometres within the Mediterranean region.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Trade in the Mediterranean during the Neolithic of Europe was greatest in this material.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-John&#95;Robb&#95;24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-John_Robb-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Networks were in existence at around 12,000 BCE<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Andrea&#95;30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Andrea-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Anatolia was the source primarily for trade with the Levant, Iran and Egypt according to Zarins study of 1990.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pliny&#95;the&#95;Elder&#95;33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pliny_the_Elder-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Melos" class="mw-redirect" title="Melos">Melos</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lipari" title="Lipari">Lipari</a> sources produced among the most widespread trading in the Mediterranean region as known to archaeology.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-E&#95;Blake,&#95;A&#95;B&#95;Knapp&#95;34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-E_Blake,_A_B_Knapp-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Sar-i_Sang" title="Sar-i Sang">Sari-i-Sang</a> mine in the mountains of Afghanistan was the largest source for trade of <a href="/wiki/Lapis_lazuli" title="Lapis lazuli">lapis lazuli</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The material was most largely traded during the <a href="/wiki/Kassites" title="Kassites">Kassite period</a> of Babylonia beginning 1595 BCE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Adam Smith traces the origins of commerce to the very start of <a href="/wiki/Financial_transaction" title="Financial transaction">transactions</a> in <a href="/wiki/Prehistoric" class="mw-redirect" title="Prehistoric">prehistoric</a> times. Apart from traditional <a href="/wiki/Self-sufficiency" class="mw-redirect" title="Self-sufficiency">self-sufficiency</a>, trading became a principal <a href="/wiki/Cognitive_skill" title="Cognitive skill">faculty</a> for prehistoric people, who <a href="/wiki/Barter" title="Barter">bartered</a> what they had for goods and services from each other. Anthropologists have found no evidence of barter systems that did not exist alongside systems of credit.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Ancient_history">Ancient history</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Ancient history"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Mediterranean_and_Near_East">Mediterranean and Near East</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Mediterranean and Near East"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/History_of_writing" title="History of writing"> earliest evidence of writing</a> is deeply bound up with trade, as <a href="/wiki/History_of_ancient_numeral_systems#Clay_tokens" title="History of ancient numeral systems">a system of clay tokens</a> used for accounting – found in the Upper Euphrates valley in Syria dated to the 10th millennium BCE – is one of the earliest surviving versions of writing. 
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ebla" title="Ebla">Ebla</a> was a prominent trading-center during the third millennium BCE, with a network reaching into Anatolia and north Mesopotamia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-E&#95;Blake,&#95;A&#95;B&#95;Knapp&#95;34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-E_Blake,_A_B_Knapp-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Laura&#95;S.&#95;Etheredge&#95;39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Laura_S._Etheredge-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Dumper&#95;&amp;&#95;Stanley&#95;40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dumper_&amp;_Stanley-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Silk_route.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Silk_route.jpg/250px-Silk_route.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Silk_route.jpg/500px-Silk_route.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2868" data-file-height="1866" /></a><figcaption>A map of the <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> trade route between Europe and Asia</figcaption></figure>
<p>Materials used for making <a href="/wiki/Jewelry" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewelry">jewelry</a> were traded with Egypt from 3000 BCE.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Long-range <a href="/wiki/Trade_route" title="Trade route">trade routes</a> first appeared in the 3rd millennium BCE, when <a href="/wiki/Sumer" title="Sumer">Sumerians</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a> traded with the <a href="/wiki/Harappan_civilization" class="mw-redirect" title="Harappan civilization">Harappan civilization</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Indus_River" title="Indus River">Indus Valley</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Phoenicians" class="mw-redirect" title="Phoenicians">Phoenicians</a> were noted maritime traders, traveling across the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea" title="Mediterranean Sea">Mediterranean Sea</a> and as far north as <a href="/wiki/Prehistoric_Britain" title="Prehistoric Britain">Britain</a> for sources of <a href="/wiki/Tin" title="Tin">tin</a> to manufacture <a href="/wiki/Bronze" title="Bronze">bronze</a>. For this purpose they established trading <a href="/wiki/Colony" title="Colony"> colonies</a> — which the Greeks called <a href="/wiki/Emporia_(ancient_Greece)" class="mw-redirect" title="Emporia (ancient Greece)">emporia</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-archa&#95;43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archa-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Along the coasts of the Mediterranean, researchers have found a positive relationship between how well-connected a coastal location was and the local prevalence of archaeological sites from the Iron Age. This suggests that a location's trade potential was an important determinant of human settlements.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Complaint_tablet_to_Ea-n%C4%81%E1%B9%A3ir" title="Complaint tablet to Ea-nāṣir">complaint tablet to Ea-nāṣir</a>, dated 1750 BCE, documents the tribulations of a copper merchant at the time.
</p><p>From the beginning of Greek <a href="/wiki/Civilization" title="Civilization">civilization</a> until the fall of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a> in the 5th century CE, a financially lucrative trade brought valuable <a href="/wiki/Spice" title="Spice">spice</a> to Europe from the far east, including India and China. <a href="/wiki/Roman_commerce" title="Roman commerce">Roman commerce</a> allowed its empire to flourish and endure, notable by importing grain to Italy from Sicily<sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
and from Egypt.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
The latter Roman Republic and the <a href="/wiki/Pax_Romana" title="Pax Romana">Pax Romana</a> of the Roman empire produced a stable and secure transportation-network that enabled the shipment of trade goods without fear of significant <a href="/wiki/Piracy" title="Piracy">piracy</a>, as Rome had become the sole effective sea-power in the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean" class="mw-redirect" title="Mediterranean">Mediterranean</a> with the conquest of Egypt and the near east.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In ancient Greece <a href="/wiki/Hermes" title="Hermes">Hermes</a> was the god of trade<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> (commerce) and weights and measures.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In ancient Rome, <i><a href="/wiki/Mercury_(mythology)" title="Mercury (mythology)">Mercurius</a></i> was the god of merchants, whose festival was celebrated by traders on the 25th day of the fifth month.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The concept of free trade was an antithesis to the will and economic direction of the sovereigns of the ancient Greek states. Free trade between states was stifled by the need for strict internal controls (via taxation) to maintain security within the treasury of the sovereign, which nevertheless enabled the maintenance of a <i><a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/modicum" class="extiw" title="wikt:modicum">modicum</a></i> of civility within the structures of functional community life.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The fall of the Roman empire and the succeeding <a href="/wiki/Dark_Ages_(historiography)" title="Dark Ages (historiography)">Dark Ages</a> brought instability to <a href="/wiki/Western_Europe" title="Western Europe">Western Europe</a> and a near-collapse of the trade network in the western world. Trade, however, continued to flourish among the kingdoms of Africa, the Middle East, India, China, and Southeast Asia. Some trade did occur in the west. For instance, <a href="/wiki/Radhanite" title="Radhanite">Radhanites</a> were a medieval guild or group (the precise meaning of the word is lost to history) of <a href="/wiki/Jew" class="mw-redirect" title="Jew">Jewish</a> merchants who traded between the <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christians</a> in Europe and the <a href="/wiki/Muslim" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim">Muslims</a> of the Near East.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Indo-Pacific">Indo-Pacific</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Indo-Pacific"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road" title="Maritime Silk Road">Maritime Silk Road</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png/330px-Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png/500px-Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png/960px-Austronesian_maritime_trade_network_in_the_Indian_Ocean.png 2x" data-file-width="2895" data-file-height="2100" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Austronesian_peoples" title="Austronesian peoples">Austronesian</a> proto-historic and <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road" title="Maritime Silk Road">historic</a> maritime trade network in the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Ocean" title="Indian Ocean">Indian Ocean</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Manguin2016&#95;56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Manguin2016-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The first true maritime trade network in the Indian Ocean was by the <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_peoples" title="Austronesian peoples">Austronesian peoples</a> of <a href="/wiki/Island_Southeast_Asia" class="mw-redirect" title="Island Southeast Asia">Island Southeast Asia</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Manguin2016&#95;56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Manguin2016-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Initiated by the indigenous peoples of <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Maritime_Jade_Road&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Maritime Jade Road (page does not exist)">Maritime Jade Road</a> was an extensive trading network connecting multiple areas in Southeast and East Asia. Its primary products were made of jade mined from Taiwan by <a href="/wiki/Taiwanese_indigenous_peoples" title="Taiwanese indigenous peoples">Taiwanese indigenous peoples</a> and processed mostly in the Philippines by indigenous Filipinos, especially in <a href="/wiki/Batanes" title="Batanes">Batanes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Luzon" title="Luzon">Luzon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Palawan" title="Palawan">Palawan</a>. Some were also processed in <a href="/wiki/Vietnam" title="Vietnam">Vietnam</a>, while the peoples of <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Brunei" title="Brunei">Brunei</a>, <a href="/wiki/Singapore" title="Singapore">Singapore</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thailand" title="Thailand">Thailand</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cambodia" title="Cambodia">Cambodia</a> also participated in the massive trading network. The maritime road is one of the most extensive sea-based trade networks of a single geological material in the prehistoric world. It was in existence for at least 3,000 years, where its peak production was from 2000 BCE to 500 CE, older than the <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> in mainland Eurasia and the later <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road" title="Maritime Silk Road">Maritime Silk Road</a>. The Maritime Jade Road began to wane during its final centuries from 500 CE until 1000 CE. The entire period of the network was a golden age for the diverse societies of the region.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Sea-faring Southeast Asians also established trade routes with <a href="/wiki/Southern_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Southern India">Southern India</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sri_Lanka" title="Sri Lanka">Sri Lanka</a> as early as 1500 BC, ushering an exchange of material culture (like <a href="/wiki/Catamaran" title="Catamaran">catamarans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Outrigger_boat" title="Outrigger boat">outrigger boats</a>, sewn-plank boats, and paan) and <a href="/wiki/Cultigen" title="Cultigen">cultigens</a> (like <a href="/wiki/Coconut" title="Coconut">coconuts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sandalwood" title="Sandalwood">sandalwood</a>, <a href="/wiki/Banana" title="Banana">bananas</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Sugarcane" title="Sugarcane">sugarcane</a>); as well as connecting the material cultures of India and China. <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Indonesia" title="Ethnic groups in Indonesia">Indonesians</a>, in particular were trading in spices (mainly <a href="/wiki/Cinnamon" title="Cinnamon">cinnamon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cassia_bark" class="mw-redirect" title="Cassia bark">cassia</a>) with <a href="/wiki/East_Africa" title="East Africa">East Africa</a> using <a href="/wiki/Catamaran" title="Catamaran">catamaran</a> and <a href="/wiki/Outrigger_boat" title="Outrigger boat">outrigger boats</a> and sailing with the help of the <a href="/wiki/Westerlies" title="Westerlies">Westerlies</a> in the Indian Ocean. This trade network expanded to reach as far as Africa and the <a href="/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula" title="Arabian Peninsula">Arabian Peninsula</a>, resulting in the Austronesian colonization of <a href="/wiki/Madagascar" title="Madagascar">Madagascar</a> by the first half of the first millennium AD. It continued up to historic times, later becoming the Maritime Silk Road.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Manguin2016&#95;56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Manguin2016-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Doran1974&#95;61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Doran1974-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Mahdi1999&#95;62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mahdi1999-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Doran1981&#95;63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Doran1981-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-BlenchFruits&#95;64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BlenchFruits-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Mesoamerica">Mesoamerica</h4><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Mesoamerica"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dinero_azada_de_Mexico_(siglos_XIV-XV).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Dinero_azada_de_Mexico_%28siglos_XIV-XV%29.jpg/250px-Dinero_azada_de_Mexico_%28siglos_XIV-XV%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Dinero_azada_de_Mexico_%28siglos_XIV-XV%29.jpg/500px-Dinero_azada_de_Mexico_%28siglos_XIV-XV%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="5675" data-file-height="4638" /></a><figcaption><i>Tajadero</i> or <a href="/wiki/Axe-monies" title="Axe-monies">axe money</a> used as currency in <a href="/wiki/Mesoamerica" title="Mesoamerica">Mesoamerica</a>. It had a fixed worth of 8,000 <a href="/wiki/Theobroma_cacao" title="Theobroma cacao">cacao</a> seeds, which were also used as currency.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The emergence of exchange networks in the Pre-Columbian societies of and near to Mexico are known to have occurred within recent years before and after 1500 BCE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Trade networks reached north to <a href="/wiki/Oasisamerica" title="Oasisamerica">Oasisamerica</a>. There is evidence of established maritime trade with the cultures of northwestern South America and the Caribbean.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Middle_Ages">Middle Ages</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Middle Ages"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>, commerce developed in Europe by trading <a href="/wiki/Luxury_goods" title="Luxury goods">luxury goods</a> at trade fairs. Wealth became converted into movable wealth or <a href="/wiki/Capital_(economics)" title="Capital (economics)">capital</a>. Banking systems developed where money on account was transferred across national boundaries. Hand-to-hand markets became a feature of town life and were regulated by town authorities.
</p><p>
Western Europe established a complex and expansive trade network with cargo ships being the main carrier of goods; <a href="/wiki/Cog_(ship)" title="Cog (ship)"> cogs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hulk_(medieval_ship_type)" title="Hulk (medieval ship type)"> hulks</a> are two examples of such cargo ships.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Many ports would develop their own extensive trade networks. The English port city of <a href="/wiki/Bristol" title="Bristol">Bristol</a> traded with peoples from Iceland, all along the western coast of France, and south to present-day Spain.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Late_Medieval_Trade_Routes.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Late_Medieval_Trade_Routes.jpg/960px-Late_Medieval_Trade_Routes.jpg" decoding="async" width="510" height="381" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Late_Medieval_Trade_Routes.jpg/1280px-Late_Medieval_Trade_Routes.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1457" data-file-height="1088" /></a><figcaption>A map showing the main <a href="/wiki/Trade_route" title="Trade route">trade routes</a> for goods within <a href="/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages" title="Late Middle Ages">late-medieval Europe</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>During the Middle Ages, Central Asia was the economic center of the world.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Beckwith2011&#95;xxiv&#95;69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Beckwith2011_xxiv-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Sogdiana" class="mw-redirect" title="Sogdiana">Sogdians</a> dominated the east–west trade-route known as the <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> from after the 4th century CE until the 8th century CE, with <a href="/wiki/Suyab" title="Suyab">Suyab</a> and <a href="/wiki/Taraz" title="Taraz">Talas</a> ranking among their main centers in the north. Sogdians functioned as the main <a href="/wiki/Caravan_(travelers)" class="mw-redirect" title="Caravan (travelers)">caravan</a> merchants of Central Asia.
</p><p>From the Middle Ages, the <a href="/wiki/Maritime_republics" title="Maritime republics">maritime republics</a>, in particular <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venice</a>, <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Pisa" title="Republic of Pisa">Pisa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Genoa</a>, played a key role in trade in the Mediterranean. From the 11th to the late-15th centuries, the <a href="/wiki/Venetian_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Venetian Republic">Venetian Republic</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa" title="Republic of Genoa">Republic of Genoa</a> were major trade-centers. They dominated trade in the Mediterranean and the Black Sea, maintaining a trading monopoly between Europe and the Near East for centuries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>From the 8th to the 11th centuries, the <a href="/wiki/Viking" class="mw-redirect" title="Viking">Vikings</a> and <a href="/wiki/Varangians" title="Varangians">Varangians</a> traded as they sailed from and to Scandinavia. Vikings sailed to Western Europe, while <a href="/wiki/Route_from_the_Varangians_to_the_Greeks" title="Route from the Varangians to the Greeks"> Varangians travelled</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27" title="Kievan Rus&#39;">Kyivan Rus'</a> and to the Black and Caspian Seas. The <a href="/wiki/Hanseatic_League" title="Hanseatic League">Hanseatic League</a>, an alliance of trading cities, maintained a trade <a href="/wiki/Monopoly" title="Monopoly">monopoly</a> over most of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Europe" title="Northern Europe">Northern Europe</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Baltic_region" title="Baltic region">Baltic</a> between the 13th and 17th centuries.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="The_Age_of_Sail_and_the_Industrial_Revolution">The Age of Sail and the Industrial Revolution</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: The Age of Sail and the Industrial Revolution"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Portuguese explorer <a href="/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama" title="Vasco da Gama">Vasco da Gama</a> pioneered the European <a href="/wiki/Spice_trade" title="Spice trade">spice trade</a> in 1498 when he reached <a href="/wiki/History_of_Kozhikode" class="mw-redirect" title="History of Kozhikode">Calicut</a> after sailing around the <a href="/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope" title="Cape of Good Hope">Cape of Good Hope</a> at the southern tip of the African continent. Prior to this, the flow of spice into Europe from India was controlled by Islamic powers, especially Egypt. The spice trade was of major economic importance and helped spur the <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Discovery" title="Age of Discovery">Age of Discovery</a> in Europe. Spices brought to Europe from the Eastern world were some of the most valuable commodities for their weight, sometimes rivaling <a href="/wiki/Gold" title="Gold">gold</a>.
</p><p>From 1070 onward, kingdoms in West <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a> became <a href="/wiki/Economic_history_of_Africa" title="Economic history of Africa">significant members of global trade</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This came initially through the movement of gold and other resources sent out by <a href="/wiki/Muslims" title="Muslims">Muslim</a> traders on the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Saharan_trade" title="Trans-Saharan trade">Trans-Saharan trading</a> network.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Beginning in the 16th century, European merchants would purchase gold, spices, cloth, timber and <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_slave_trade" title="Atlantic slave trade">slaves</a> from West African states as part of the <a href="/wiki/Triangular_trade" title="Triangular trade">triangular trade</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This was often in exchange for <a href="/wiki/Cloth" class="mw-redirect" title="Cloth">cloth</a>, <a href="/wiki/Iron" title="Iron">iron</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Cowrie_shells" class="mw-redirect" title="Cowrie shells">cowrie shells</a> which were used locally as currency.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Founded in 1352, the <a href="/wiki/Bengal_Sultanate" title="Bengal Sultanate">Bengal Sultanate</a> was a major <a href="/wiki/Trading_nation" title="Trading nation">trading nation</a> in the world and often referred to by Europeans as the wealthiest country with which to trade.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the 16th and 17th centuries, the Portuguese gained an economic advantage in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kongo" title="Kingdom of Kongo">Kingdom of Kongo</a> due to different philosophies of trade.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Whereas Portuguese traders concentrated on the accumulation of capital, in Kongo spiritual meaning was attached to many objects of trade. According to economic historian <a href="/wiki/Toby_Green" title="Toby Green">Toby Green</a>, in Kongo "giving more than receiving was a symbol of spiritual and political power and privilege."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;71-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the 16th century, the <a href="/wiki/Seventeen_Provinces" title="Seventeen Provinces">Seventeen Provinces</a> were the center of free trade, imposing no <a href="/wiki/Exchange_control" class="mw-redirect" title="Exchange control">exchange controls</a>, and advocating the free movement of goods. Trade in the <a href="/wiki/East_Indies" title="East Indies">East Indies</a> was dominated by Portugal in the 16th century, the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a> in the 17th century, and the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">British</a> in the 18th century. The <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a> developed regular trade links across both the Atlantic and the Pacific Oceans.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg/330px-Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="202" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg/500px-Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/53/Wojciech_Gerson_-_Gda%C5%84sk_in_the_XVII_century.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="558" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Danzig" class="mw-redirect" title="Danzig">Danzig</a> in the 17th century, a port of the <a href="/wiki/Hanseatic_League" title="Hanseatic League">Hanseatic League</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1776, <a href="/wiki/Adam_Smith" title="Adam Smith">Adam Smith</a> published the paper <i><a href="/wiki/An_Inquiry_into_the_Nature_and_Causes_of_the_Wealth_of_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations">An Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations</a></i>. It criticized <a href="/wiki/Mercantilism" title="Mercantilism">Mercantilism</a>, and argued that <a href="/wiki/Economic" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic">economic</a> specialization could benefit nations just as much as firms. Since the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_labour" title="Division of labour">division of labour</a> was restricted by the size of the market, he said that countries having access to larger markets would be able to divide labour more efficiently and thereby become more <a href="/wiki/Productive" class="mw-redirect" title="Productive">productive</a>. Smith said that he considered all rationalizations of <a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">import</a> and <a href="/wiki/Export" title="Export">export</a> controls "dupery", which hurt the trading nation as a whole for the benefit of specific industries.
</p><p>In 1799, the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company" title="Dutch East India Company">Dutch East India Company</a>, formerly the world's largest company, became <a href="/wiki/Bankrupt" class="mw-redirect" title="Bankrupt">bankrupt</a>, partly due to the rise of competitive free trade.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg/330px-Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg/500px-Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg/960px-Berber_Trade_with_Timbuktu_1300s.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1012" data-file-height="713" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Berber_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Berber people">Berber</a> trade with <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a>, 1853</figcaption></figure>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="19th_century">19th century</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: 19th century"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>In 1817, <a href="/wiki/David_Ricardo" title="David Ricardo">David Ricardo</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_Mill" title="James Mill">James Mill</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Torrens_(economist)" title="Robert Torrens (economist)">Robert Torrens</a> showed that free trade would benefit the industrially weak as well as the strong, in the famous theory of <a href="/wiki/Comparative_advantage" title="Comparative advantage">comparative advantage</a>. In <a href="/wiki/Principles_of_Political_Economy_and_Taxation" class="mw-redirect" title="Principles of Political Economy and Taxation">Principles of Political Economy and Taxation</a> Ricardo advanced the doctrine still considered the most counterintuitive in <a href="/wiki/Economics" title="Economics">economics</a>:
</p>
<dl><dd><i>When an inefficient producer sends the merchandise it produces best to a country able to produce it more efficiently, both countries benefit.</i></dd></dl>
<p>The ascendancy of free trade was primarily based on national advantage in the mid 19th century. That is, the calculation made was whether it was in any particular country's self-interest to open its borders to imports.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill" title="John Stuart Mill">John Stuart Mill</a> proved that a country with monopoly <a href="/wiki/Pricing_power" class="mw-redirect" title="Pricing power">pricing power</a> on the international market could manipulate the <a href="/wiki/Terms_of_trade" title="Terms of trade">terms of trade</a> through maintaining <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a>, and that the response to this might be <a href="/wiki/Reciprocity_(international_relations)" title="Reciprocity (international relations)">reciprocity</a> in trade policy. Ricardo and others had suggested this earlier. This was taken as evidence against the universal doctrine of free trade, as it was believed that more of the <a href="/wiki/Economic_surplus" title="Economic surplus">economic surplus</a> of trade would accrue to a country following <i>reciprocal</i>, rather than completely free, trade policies. This was followed within a few years by the <a href="/wiki/Infant_industry" title="Infant industry">infant industry</a> scenario developed by Mill promoting the theory that the government had the duty to <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protect</a> young industries, although only for a time necessary for them to develop full capacity. This became the policy in many countries attempting to <a href="/wiki/Industrialize" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrialize">industrialize</a> and out-compete English exporters. <a href="/wiki/Milton_Friedman" title="Milton Friedman">Milton Friedman</a> later continued this vein of thought, showing that in a few circumstances tariffs might be beneficial to the host country; but never for the world at large.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="20th_century">20th century</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 20th century"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a> was a major economic recession that ran from 1929 to the late 1930s. During this period, there was a great drop in trade and other economic indicators.
</p><p>The lack of free trade was considered by many as a principal cause of the depression causing stagnation and inflation.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Only during <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> did the recession end in the United States. Also during the war, in 1944, 44 countries signed the <a href="/wiki/Bretton_Woods_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Bretton Woods Agreement">Bretton Woods Agreement</a>, intended to prevent national trade barriers, to avoid depressions. It set up rules and institutions to regulate the <a href="/wiki/International_political_economy" title="International political economy">international political economy</a>: the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a> and the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (later divided into the World Bank $ Bank for International Settlements). These organizations became operational in 1946 after enough countries ratified the agreement. In 1947, 23 countries agreed to the <a href="/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Tariffs_and_Trade" title="General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade">General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade</a> to promote free trade.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> became the world's largest exporter of manufactured goods and services, the biggest export market for around 80 countries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="21st_century">21st century</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 21st century"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization">Globalization</a></div>
<p>Today, trade is merely a subset within a complex system of <a href="/wiki/Corporation" title="Corporation">companies</a> which try to maximize their profits by offering <a href="/wiki/Product_(business)" title="Product (business)">products</a> and <a href="/wiki/Service_(economics)" title="Service (economics)">services</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Market_(economics)" title="Market (economics)">market</a> (which consists both of individuals and other companies) at the lowest <a href="/wiki/Production_cost" class="mw-redirect" title="Production cost">production cost</a>. A system of <a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">international trade</a> has helped to develop the world economy but, in combination with bilateral or multilateral agreements to lower <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> or to achieve <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>, has sometimes harmed <a href="/wiki/Third_World" title="Third World">third-world markets</a> for local products.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Free_trade">Free trade</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Free trade"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">Free trade</a></div>
<p>Free trade is a policy by which a government does not discriminate against imports or exports by applying tariffs or subsidies. This policy is also known as laissez-faire policy. This kind of policy does not necessarily imply a country will then abandon all control and taxation of imports and exports.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Free trade advanced further in the late 20th century and early 2000s:
</p>
<ul><li>1992 <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> lifted barriers to internal trade in <a href="/wiki/Good_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Good (economics)">goods</a> and <a href="/wiki/Labour_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Labour (economics)">labour</a>.</li>
<li>January 1, 1994 the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a> (NAFTA) took effect.</li>
<li>1994 The GATT <a href="/wiki/Marrakech_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Marrakech Agreement">Marrakech Agreement</a> specified formation of the WTO.</li>
<li>January 1, 1995 <a href="/wiki/World_Trade_Organization" title="World Trade Organization">World Trade Organization</a> was created to facilitate <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>, by mandating mutual <a href="/wiki/Most_favored_nation" class="mw-redirect" title="Most favored nation">most favored nation</a> trading status between all signatories.</li>
<li>EC was transformed into the European Union, which accomplished the Economic and Monetary Union (EMU) in 2002, through introducing the Euro, and creating this way a real single market between 13 member states as of January 1, 2007.</li>
<li><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Accarias_de_S%C3%A9rionne_-_Int%C3%A9r%C3%AAts_des_nations_de_l%27Europe,_d%C3%A9v%C3%A9lop%C3%A9s_relativement_au_commerce,_1766_-_5790093.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Accarias_de_S%C3%A9rionne_-_Int%C3%A9r%C3%AAts_des_nations_de_l%27Europe%2C_d%C3%A9v%C3%A9lop%C3%A9s_relativement_au_commerce%2C_1766_-_5790093.tif/lossy-page1-250px-Accarias_de_S%C3%A9rionne_-_Int%C3%A9r%C3%AAts_des_nations_de_l%27Europe%2C_d%C3%A9v%C3%A9lop%C3%A9s_relativement_au_commerce%2C_1766_-_5790093.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="357" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Accarias_de_S%C3%A9rionne_-_Int%C3%A9r%C3%AAts_des_nations_de_l%27Europe%2C_d%C3%A9v%C3%A9lop%C3%A9s_relativement_au_commerce%2C_1766_-_5790093.tif/lossy-page1-500px-Accarias_de_S%C3%A9rionne_-_Int%C3%A9r%C3%AAts_des_nations_de_l%27Europe%2C_d%C3%A9v%C3%A9lop%C3%A9s_relativement_au_commerce%2C_1766_-_5790093.tif.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2768" data-file-height="3954" /></a><figcaption><i>Intérêts des nations de l'Europe, dévélopés relativement au commerce</i> (1766)</figcaption></figure>2005, the <a href="/wiki/Central_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Central American Free Trade Agreement">Central American Free Trade Agreement</a> was signed; It includes the United States and the Dominican Republic.</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Perspectives">Perspectives</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Perspectives"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Protectionism">Protectionism</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Protectionism"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">Protectionism</a></div>
<p>Protectionism is the policy of restraining and discouraging trade between states and contrasts with the policy of free trade. This policy often takes the form of <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> and restrictive <a href="/wiki/Import_quota" title="Import quota">quotas</a>. Protectionist policies were particularly prevalent in the 1930s, between the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a> and the onset of World War II.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Religion">Religion</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Religion"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Islamic teachings encourage trading.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>79<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Judeao-Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Judeao-Christian">Judeao-Christian</a> teachings do not prohibit trade. They do prohibit fraud and dishonest measures. 
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Development_of_money">Development of money</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Development of money"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_money" title="History of money">History of money</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Roman_denarius_in_silver_(Maximinus)-transparent.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/35/Roman_denarius_in_silver_%28Maximinus%29-transparent.png" decoding="async" width="219" height="203" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="219" data-file-height="203" /></a><figcaption>A Roman <a href="/wiki/Denarius" title="Denarius">denarius</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The first instances of money were objects with intrinsic value. This is called <a href="/wiki/Commodity_money" title="Commodity money">commodity money</a> and includes any commonly available commodity that has intrinsic value; historical examples include pigs, rare seashells, whale's teeth, and (often) cattle. In medieval Iraq, bread was used as an early form of money. In the <a href="/wiki/Aztec_Empire" title="Aztec Empire">Aztec Empire</a>, under the rule of <a href="/wiki/Moctezuma_II" title="Moctezuma II">Montezuma</a> cocoa beans became legitimate currency.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>80<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Currency" title="Currency">Currency</a> was introduced as standardised money to facilitate a wider exchange of goods and services. This first stage of currency, where metals were used to represent stored value, and symbols to represent commodities, formed the basis of trade in the Fertile Crescent for over 1500 years.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Numismatist" title="Numismatist">Numismatists</a> have examples of coins from the earliest large-scale societies, although these were initially unmarked lumps of <a href="/wiki/Precious_metal" title="Precious metal">precious metal</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-originsofmoney&#95;81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-originsofmoney-81"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>81<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Trends">Trends</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Trends"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Doha_rounds">Doha rounds</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Doha rounds"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Doha_round" class="mw-redirect" title="Doha round">Doha round</a></div>
<p>The Doha round of World Trade Organization negotiations aimed to lower <a href="/wiki/Trade_barrier" title="Trade barrier">barriers to trade</a> around the world, with a focus on making <a href="/wiki/Fair_trade" title="Fair trade">trade more fair</a> for <a href="/wiki/Developing_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Developing countries">developing countries</a>. Talks have been hung over a divide between the rich <a href="/wiki/Developed_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Developed countries">developed countries</a>, represented by the <a href="/wiki/G20" title="G20">G20</a>, and the major developing countries. <a href="/wiki/Agricultural_subsidies" class="mw-redirect" title="Agricultural subsidies">Agricultural subsidies</a> are the most significant issue upon which agreement has been the hardest to negotiate. By contrast, there was much agreement on <a href="/wiki/Trade_facilitation" title="Trade facilitation">trade facilitation</a> and capacity building. The Doha round began in <a href="/wiki/Doha" title="Doha">Doha</a>, Qatar.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="China">China</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: China"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/China_shock" title="China shock">China shock</a>, <a href="/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up" title="Reform and opening up">Reform and opening up</a>, and <a href="/wiki/China_and_the_World_Trade_Organization" title="China and the World Trade Organization">China and the World Trade Organization</a></div>
<p>Beginning around 1978, the government of the <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_China" class="mw-redirect" title="People&#39;s Republic of China">People's Republic of China</a> (PRC) began the <a href="/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up" title="Reform and opening up">reform and opening up</a>. In contrast to the previous <a href="/wiki/USSR" class="mw-redirect" title="USSR">Soviet</a>-style <a href="/wiki/Centrally_planned_economy" class="mw-redirect" title="Centrally planned economy">centrally planned economy</a>, the new measures progressively relaxed restrictions on farming, agricultural distribution and, several years later, urban enterprises and labor. The more market-oriented approach reduced inefficiencies and stimulated private investment, particularly by farmers, which led to increased productivity and output. One feature was the establishment of four (later five) <a href="/wiki/Special_Economic_Zone" class="mw-redirect" title="Special Economic Zone">Special Economic Zones</a> located along the South-east coast.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>83<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The reforms proved spectacularly successful in terms of increased output, variety, quality, <a href="/wiki/Price" title="Price">price</a> and <a href="/wiki/Demand" title="Demand">demand</a>. In real terms, the economy doubled in size between 1978 and 1986, doubled again by 1994, and again by 2003. On a real per capita basis, doubling from the 1978 base took place in 1987, 1996 and 2006. By 2008, the economy was 16.7 times the size it was in 1978, and 12.1 times its previous per capita levels. International trade progressed even more rapidly, doubling on average every 4.5 years. Total two-way trade in January 1998 exceeded that for all of 1978; in the first quarter of 2009, trade exceeded the full-year 1998 level. In 2008, China's two-way trade totaled US$2.56 trillion.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>84<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In 1991 China joined the <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation</a> group, a trade-promotion forum.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>85<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 2001, it also joined the World Trade Organization.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>86<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="International_trade">International trade</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: International trade"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">International trade</a> and <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_trade" title="Balance of trade">Balance of trade</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1246091330" /><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks" role="navigation"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle"><i>Part of <a href="/wiki/Category:Trade" title="Category:Trade">a series</a> on</i></td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">World trade</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image" style="padding: 0.4em 0 0.4em; border-bottom: 1px solid #ccc"><figure class="mw-halign-center notpageimage" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg/250px-Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="181" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg/330px-Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg/500px-Storck_Harbour_scene.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1590" data-file-height="1599" /></a><figcaption></figcaption></figure></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="border-bottom: 1px solid #ccc; background: transparent; text-align: left;;color: var(--color-base)"><a href="/wiki/Commercial_policy" title="Commercial policy">Policy</a></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Import" title="Import">Import</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Export" title="Export">Export</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Balance_of_trade" title="Balance of trade">Balance of trade</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/International_trade_law" title="International trade law">Trade law</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Trade_agreement" title="Trade agreement">Trade pact</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Trade_bloc" title="Trade bloc">Trade bloc</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Trade_creation" title="Trade creation">Trade creation</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Trade_diversion" title="Trade diversion">Trade diversion</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Export-oriented_industrialization" title="Export-oriented industrialization">Export orientation</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Import_substitution_industrialization" title="Import substitution industrialization">Import substitution</a></li>
