</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" /><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1239400231" /><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:World_trade" title="Template:World trade"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:World_trade" title="Template talk:World trade"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:World_trade" title="Special:EditPage/Template:World trade"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">International trade</a> is the exchange of goods and services across national borders. In most countries, it represents a significant part of <a href="/wiki/Gross_Domestic_Product" class="mw-redirect" title="Gross Domestic Product">GDP</a>. While international trade has been present throughout much of history (see Silk Road, <a href="/wiki/Amber_Road" title="Amber Road">Amber Road</a>), its economic, social, and political importance have increased in recent centuries, mainly because of <a href="/wiki/Industrialization" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrialization">Industrialization</a>, advanced <a href="/wiki/Transportation" class="mw-redirect" title="Transportation">transportation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization">globalization</a>, <a href="/wiki/Multinational_corporation" title="Multinational corporation">multinational corporations</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Outsourcing" title="Outsourcing">outsourcing</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Empirical evidence for the success of trade can be seen in the contrast between countries such as <a href="/wiki/South_Korea" title="South Korea">South Korea</a>, which adopted a policy of <a href="/wiki/Export-oriented_industrialization" title="Export-oriented industrialization">export-oriented industrialization</a>, and India, which historically had a more closed policy. South Korea has done much better by economic criteria than India over the past fifty years, though its success also has to do with effective state institutions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>87<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Trade_sanctions">Trade sanctions</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Trade sanctions"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Trade_sanctions" class="mw-redirect" title="Trade sanctions">Trade sanctions</a> against a specific country are sometimes imposed, in order to punish that country for some action. An <a href="/wiki/Embargo" class="mw-redirect" title="Embargo">embargo</a>, a severe form of externally imposed isolation, is a blockade of all trade by one country on another. For example, the United States has had an <a href="/wiki/United_States_embargo_against_Cuba" title="United States embargo against Cuba">embargo</a> against <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a> for over 60 years.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>88<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Embargoes are usually on a temporary basis. For example, <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a> put a temporary embargo on Turkish products and bans any imports from Turkey on December 31, 2020. The situation is prompted by food security concerns given Turkey's hostile attitude towards Armenia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>89<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Fair_trade">Fair trade</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Trade&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Fair trade"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>The "<a href="/wiki/Fair_trade" title="Fair trade">fair trade</a>" movement, also known as the "trade justice" movement, promotes the use of <a href="/wiki/Manual_labour" title="Manual labour">labour</a>, <a href="/wiki/Environmental_movement" title="Environmental movement">environmental</a> and <a href="/wiki/Social_issues" class="mw-redirect" title="Social issues">social</a> standards for the production of goods, particularly those exported from the <a href="/wiki/Third_World" title="Third World">Third</a> and <a href="/wiki/Second_World" title="Second World">Second Worlds</a> to the <a href="/wiki/First_World" title="First World">First World</a>. Such ideas have also sparked a debate on whether trade itself should be codified as a <a href="/wiki/Human_right" class="mw-redirect" title="Human right">human right</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>90<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Importing firms voluntarily adhere to fair trade standards or governments may enforce them through a combination of <a href="/wiki/Employment_law" class="mw-redirect" title="Employment law">employment</a> and <a href="/wiki/Commercial_law" title="Commercial law">commercial law</a>. Proposed and practiced fair trade policies vary widely, ranging from the common prohibition of <a href="/wiki/Good_(economics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Good (economics)">goods</a> made using <a href="/wiki/Slave_labour" class="mw-redirect" title="Slave labour">slave labour</a> to minimum <a href="/wiki/Price_support" title="Price support">price support</a> schemes such as those for coffee in the 1980s. <a href="/wiki/Non-governmental_organization" title="Non-governmental organization">Non-governmental organizations</a> also play a role in promoting fair trade standards by serving as independent monitors of compliance with labeling requirements.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>91<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>92<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> As such, it is a form of Protectionism.
</p>

<li id="cite&#95;note-Abulafia,&#95;Rackham,&#95;Suano-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Abulafia,_Rackham,_Suano_12-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFAbulafiaRackhamSuano2011" class="citation cs2">Abulafia, D.; Rackham, O.; Suano, M. This period runs for about 25,000 years, from 35,000/30,000 to around 10,000/8,000 before the present.</q></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Mediterranean+in+History&amp;rft.pub=Getty+Publications&amp;rft.date=2011&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-60606-057-5&amp;rft.aulast=Abulafia&amp;rft.aufirst=D.&amp;rft.au=Rackham%2C+O.&amp;rft.au=Suano%2C+M.&amp;rft&#95;id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3D9Cvrq-Vs-2wC&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ATrade" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite&#95;note-archa-43"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-archa_43-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFDikov2015" class="citation journal cs1">Dikov, Ivan (12 July 2015). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20150712235249/http://archaeologyinbulgaria.com/2015/07/12/bulgarian-archaeologists-to-start-excavations-of-ancient-greek-emporium-in-thracians-odrysian-kingdom/">"Bulgarian Archaeologists To Start Excavations of Ancient Greek Emporium in Thracians' the Odrysian Kingdom"</a>. <i>Archaeology in Bulgaria</i>. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">28 October</span> 2010</span>. <q>An emporium (in Latin; 'emporion' in Greek) was a settlement reserved as a trading post, usually for the Ancient Greeks, on the territory of another ancient nation, in this case, the Ancient Thracian Odrysian Kingdom (5th century BC – 1st century AD), the most powerful Thracian state.</q></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Archaeology+in+Bulgaria&amp;rft.atitle=Bulgarian+Archaeologists+To+Start+Excavations+of+Ancient+Greek+Emporium+in+Thracians%27+the+Odrysian+Kingdom&amp;rft.date=2015-07-12&amp;rft.aulast=Dikov&amp;rft.aufirst=Ivan&amp;rft&#95;id=http%3A%2F%2Farchaeologyinbulgaria.com%2F2015%2F07%2F12%2Fbulgarian-archaeologists-to-start-excavations-of-ancient-greek-emporium-in-thracians-odrysian-kingdom%2F&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ATrade" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite&#95;note-44"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-44">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Jan David Bakker, Stephan Maurer, Jörn-Steffen Pischke and Ferdinand Rauch. 2021. <li id="cite&#95;note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333433106" /><cite id="CITEREFAtkin1995" class="citation book cs1">Atkin, Michael (31 May 1995) [1992]. International Trade Series (2&#160;ed.). Abingdon, Cambridge: Woodhead Publishing. pp.&#160;<span class="nowrap">15–</span>16. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781855732025" title="Special:BookSources/9781855732025"><bdi>9781855732025</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">9 July</span> 2025</span>. <q>Egypt was Rome's principal supplier, accounting for about one third of the city's total needs. The balance of the grain came from other regions within the Mediterranean basin. [...] The ships used on the main route, from Alexandria to Rome, were 1,200 - 1,300 toms and posted an average speed of about three knots. [...] the sophistication of the grain trading system used to provision Rome was not equalled until the nineteenth century.</q></cite><span title="ctx&#95;ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft&#95;val&#95;fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=A+brief+history+of+the+grain+trade&amp;rft.btitle=The+International+Grain+Trade&amp;rft.place=Abingdon%2C+Cambridge&amp;rft.series=International+Trade+Series&amp;rft.pages=15-16&amp;rft.edition=2&amp;rft.pub=Woodhead+Publishing&amp;rft.date=1995-05-31&amp;rft.isbn=9781855732025&amp;rft.aulast=Atkin&amp;rft.aufirst=Michael&amp;rft&#95;id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DMh&#95;YuHaONo4C&amp;rfr&#95;id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ATrade" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite&#95;note-originsofmoney-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-originsofmoney_81-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Gold was an especially common form of early money, as described in <a href="#Davies2002">Davies (2002)</a>.</span>
